\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Let
\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet}
and
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}
be the leftmost column and the bottom row of
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,\bullet}
. Let
\text{Tot}(C)_{\bullet}
and
\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet}
each denote the total chain complex of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
and
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,\bullet}
. Explicitly,
\text{Tot}(C)_{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}C_{p,q}
and
\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\tilde{C}_{p,q}
. There is a short exact sequence of complexes

0\to\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}\oplus\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet}\to\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet}\to\text{Tot}(C)_{\bullet}\to 0,

which leads to the following long exact sequence

\cdots\to H_{n+1}(\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}\oplus\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet})\to H_{n}(\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet})\to H_{n}(\text{Tot}(C)_{\bullet})\to H_{n}(\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}\oplus\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet})\to\cdots.

As we observed earlier, the total complex of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
is the cellular chain complex for
K\times_{\tilde{R}}M
:

H_{n}(\text{Tot}(C)_{\bullet})=H_{n}(K\times_{\tilde{R}}M).

The leftmost column
\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet}
is the chain complex of
M
and the bottom row
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}
is the chain complex of
K
. Thus,

H_{n}(\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet}\oplus\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1})=H_{n}(M)\oplus H_{n}(K).

Furthermore, one can view
\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet}
as the cellular chain complex of the relational join complex
K\star_{\tilde{R}}M
. In particular, we can express
E^{0}_{p,q}
as

E^{0}_{p,q}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\sigma,\tau)\,\in\,K\star_{\tilde{R}}M\\
\sigma\in K_{p},\,\tau\in M_{q}\end{subarray}}G_{(\sigma,\tau)}\,.

Here,
(\sigma,\tau)
is a
(p+q+1)
-simplex of
K\star_{\tilde{R}}M
. Therefore,

H_{n}(\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet})=H_{n+1}(K\star_{\tilde{R}}M).

All together, we obtain the long exact sequence

\cdots\to H_{n+1}(K)\oplus H_{n+1}(M)\to H_{n+1}(K\star_{\tilde{R}}M)\to H_{n}(K\times_{\tilde{R}}M)\to H_{n}(K)\oplus H_{n}(M)\to\cdots.

∎

4.1.
Applications: Dowker duality

The weak version of Dowker duality follows from Theorem
4.2
. Let
R\subseteq A\times X
be a relation between sets, and let
\tilde{R}\subseteq D_{A}\times D_{X}
be the induced relation on the Dowker complexes. Let
\mathcal{F}_{A}
be the cellular cosheaf on
D_{A}
with the following assignments.

•

Local sections: For each simplex
\sigma_{A}
of
D_{A}
,
\mathcal{F}_{A}(\sigma_{A})=\sigma_{X}
is the maximal simplex of
D_{X}
such that
aRx
for all
a\in\sigma_{A}
and
x\in\sigma_{X}
.

•

Extension maps: If
\sigma
is a face of
\tau
, the extension map
\mathcal{F}_{A}(\sigma\subseteq\tau):\mathcal{F}_{A}(\tau)\to\mathcal{F}_{A}(\sigma)
is the inclusion of simplices.

The cosheaves
\mathcal{F}_{A}^{p}
are obtained by applying the
p^{\text{th}}
homology functor to
\mathcal{F}_{A}
. Note that
\mathcal{F}^{q}_{A}(\sigma)=0
for all
q>0
and all
\sigma
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{A}(\sigma)=G
for all
\sigma
, where
G
is the abelian group used as the coefficient of the homologies.

We then have the spectral sequences

E^{A,2}_{p,q}=H_{p}(D_{A},\mathcal{F}^{q}_{A})\cong\begin{cases}H_{p}(D_{A})&\quad\text{ for }q=0\\
0&\quad\text{ for }q>0\end{cases}

and

E^{X,2}_{p,q}=H_{q}(D_{X},\mathcal{F}^{p}_{X})\cong\begin{cases}H_{q}(D_{X})&\quad\text{ for }p=0\\
0&\quad\text{ for }p>0.\end{cases}

When
q=0
, the cosheaf homology
H_{p}(D_{A},\mathcal{F}^{0}_{A})
simply computes the homology of
D_{A}
with constant coefficients. Similarly,
H_{q}(D_{X},\mathcal{F}^{0}_{X})
computes the homology of
D_{X}
with constant coefficients. From Theorem
4.2
both spectral sequences converge to the homologies of
K\times_{\tilde{R}}M
. Here, both
E^{A,2}_{p,q}
and
E^{X,2}_{p,q}
collapse on the 2nd page. Then,
H_{n}(K\times_{\tilde{R}}M)
is the unique nonzero
E^{A,2}_{p,q}
with
p+q=n
and the unique nonzero
E^{X,2}_{p,q}
with
p+q=n
(
[
32
]
5.2.7). Thus,
H_{n}(D_{X})\cong H_{n}(K\times_{\tilde{R}}M)\cong H_{n}(D_{A})
.

Example 4.3
.

Recall the relation matrix from Example
3.7
.

R=\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&w&x&y&z\\
a&0&1&0&1\\
b&0&1&1&0\\
c&0&0&1&1\\
d&1&0&1&0\\
\end{array}

Figure 10.
Example cosheaves
\mathcal{F}_{A}
on
D_{A}
and
\mathcal{F}_{X}
on
D_{X}
.

D_{A}
and
D_{X}

illustrates the cellular cosheaves

and
\mathcal{F}_{X}
. The double complex
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
is shown below. Here, we use the notation
G\,S=\bigoplus_{s\in S}G_{s}
for a set
S
.

with respect to the horizontal differentials, one obtains
E^{X,2}
page which is a transpose of the above diagram. The terms in

and
E^{X,2}
are the homologies of either Dowker complexes.

4.2.
Applications: covers of simplicial complexes

We can now understand the relations between a simplicial complex and the nerve of its covering, even when the cover isn’t a good cover. Let
K
be a simplicial complex, and let
\mathcal{U}
be any covering of
K
with subcomplexes. Let
N\mathcal{U}
be the nerve of the cover, and let
\tilde{R}\subseteq K\times N\mathcal{U}
be the covering relation.

Since
\mathcal{U}
isn’t necessarily a good cover,
K
is not necessarily homotopy equivalent to
N\mathcal{U}
. However, the homologies of
K
and
N\mathcal{U}
fit into a long exact sequence according to Theorem
4.1

\cdots\to H_{n}(K)\oplus H_{n}(N\mathcal{U})\to H_{n}(K\star_{\tilde{R}}N\mathcal{U})\to H_{n}(K\times_{\tilde{R}}N\mathcal{U})\to H_{n-1}(K)\oplus H_{n-1}(N\mathcal{U})\to\cdots\;.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{K}
and
\mathcal{F}_{N\mathcal{U}}
be the corresponding cellular cosheaves. Note that
\mathcal{F}_{K}(\sigma)
is the simplex of
N\mathcal{U}
corresponding to all cover elements covering
\sigma
. The local section
\mathcal{F}_{N\mathcal{U}}(\tau)
is the simplicial complex that is covered by the elements corresponding to
\tau
. According to Theorem
4.2
, there are spectral sequences
{E^{K,2}_{p,q}=H_{p}(K,\mathcal{F}_{K}^{q})}
and
E^{N\mathcal{U},2}_{p,q}=H_{q}(N\mathcal{U},\mathcal{F}_{N\mathcal{U}}^{p})
consisting of cosheaf homologies that converge to the homology of the relational product complex:

\displaystyle E^{K,2}_{p,q}

\displaystyle\implies H_{p+q}(K\times_{\tilde{R}}N\mathcal{U})

\displaystyle E^{N\mathcal{U},2}_{p,q}

\displaystyle\implies H_{p+q}(K\times_{\tilde{R}}N\mathcal{U}).

Note that
E^{K,2}
collapses on the second page because the local sections of
\mathcal{F}_{K}
are simplices.

Example 4.4
.

Let
K
be the simplicial complex in Figure
11
A. Let
\mathcal{U}=\{x,y\}
be a covering of
K
(Figure
11
A). Let
\tilde{R}\subseteq K\times N\mathcal{U}
be the covering relation. The relational join complex and the product complex are illustrated in Figure
11
C. Their homologies fit into a long exact sequence. Figure
11
D illustrates the two cellular cosheaves
\mathcal{F}_{K}
and
\mathcal{F}_{N\mathcal{U}}
. The relevant spectral sequences converge to the homology of
K\times_{\tilde{R}}N\mathcal{U}
.

Figure 11.
Example cover of a simplicial complex.
A.
A simplicial complex
K
and a covering
\mathcal{U}
that is not a good cover.
B.

K
and the nerve
N\mathcal{U}
.
C.
The relational product complex
K\times_{\tilde{R}}N\mathcal{U}
(top) and the relational join complex
K\star_{\tilde{R}}N\mathcal{U}
(bottom).
D.
The two relevant cosheaves
\mathcal{F}_{K}
and
\mathcal{F}_{N\mathcal{U}}.

5.
Profunctors

We now generalize the constructions of relational join and relational product complexes of relations to profunctors. The analogous constructions, which are graphs and cographs, already exist in the literature. In Section
5.1
, we show that when profunctors arise from adjoint functors between
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
, then
\mathsf{C}
,
\mathsf{D}
, the graph, and cograph all have homotopy equivalent classifying spaces, generalizing Dowker duality via relational joins and relational products (Theorems
3.18
,
3.25
). In Section
5.2
, we establish a long exact sequence involving the homologies of
\mathsf{C}
,
\mathsf{D}
, and cograph for any general profunctor.

5.1.
Profunctors arising from adjoint pairs

Given an adjoint pair
{L}:\mathsf{C}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{D}:{U}
, there is an associated profunctor
P:\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}\times\mathsf{D}\to\text{Set}
, where

P(c,d)=\hom_{\mathsf{C}}(c,Ud)=\hom_{\mathsf{D}}(Lc,d).

For any heteromorphism
\varphi\in P(c,d)
, we use
\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}
to denote the corresponding morphism in
\hom_{\mathsf{C}}(c,Ud)
and
\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}
to denote the corresponding morphism in
\hom_{\mathsf{D}}(Lc,d)
. The correspondence between
\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}
arises from the adjunction.

Recall the actions of morphisms
f:c^{\prime}\to c
in
\mathsf{C}
and
g:d\to d^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{D}
on
\varphi:c\rightsquigarrow d
(Section
2.7
). The actions can also be viewed pre- and post-compositions of heteromorphisms with morphisms in categories
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
as follows. The action of
f:c^{\prime}\to c
on
\varphi
is
\varphi f:c^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow d
, shown in the diagram below (left). Such action corresponds to a composition of
\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}
with
f
in
\mathsf{C}
and composition of
\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}
with
Lf
in
\mathsf{D}
as shown below.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc^{\prime}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}\circ Lf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}}

Similarly, the action of
g:d\to d^{\prime}
on
\varphi:c\rightsquigarrow d
can be described as the following compositions in
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ug\circ\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ug}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

5.1.1.
Cographs of profunctors arising from adjoint pairs

The cograph of profunctors (Def.
2.33
) play an analogous role to the relational join poset and relational join complex from earlier sections. Given an adjoint pair
{L}:\mathsf{C}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{D}:{U}
, let
P:\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}\times\mathsf{D}\to\text{Set}
be the associated profunctor, and let
\mathsf{J}
denote the cograph of
P
. Here, the homsets of
\mathsf{J}
are

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathsf{J}}(c,c^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\hom_{\mathsf{C}}(c,c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathsf{J}}(d,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\hom_{\mathsf{D}}(d,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathsf{J}}(c,d)

\displaystyle=P(c,d)=\hom_{\mathsf{{C}}}(c,Ud)=\hom_{\mathsf{{D}}}(Lc,d)

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathsf{J}}(d,c)

\displaystyle=\emptyset\;.

In the following diagrams, the diagram in
\mathsf{J}
(left) commutes if and only if the corresponding diagrams in
\mathsf{C}
(center) and
\mathsf{D}
(right) below commutes. By adjointness, if one of the diagrams in
\mathsf{C}
or
\mathsf{D}
commutes, then the other diagram commutes as well.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}}

Similarly, the diagram in
\mathsf{J}
(left) commutes if and only if the corresponding diagrams in
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
below commutes.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\mathsf{{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ug}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\mathsf{{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

When the profunctor arises from an adjoint pair, then the classifying space of the cograph is homotopy equivalent to the classifying spaces of
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
. The following theorem is analogous to Dowker duality via relational join (Theorem
3.18
).

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
{L}:\mathsf{C}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{D}:{U}
be an adjoint pair, and let
P:\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}\times\mathsf{D}\to\text{Set}
be the associated profunctor. Let
\mathsf{J}
be the cograph of profunctor
P
. Then,

B\mathsf{C}\simeq B\mathsf{\mathsf{J}}\simeq B\mathsf{D}
.

Proof.

Let
{F}:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{J}
be the inclusion functor. We will show that the fibers
{F}{\downarrow}j
are contractible for all objects
j
of
\mathsf{J}
by showing that the fibers have terminal objects. Recall the objects and morphisms of
{F}{\downarrow}j
:

•

Objects:
(c,h)
, where
c
is an object of
\mathsf{C}
and
h:{F}(c)\to j
is a morphism in
\mathsf{J}
.

•

Morphism:
f:(c,h)\to(c^{\prime},h^{\prime})
is a morphism
f:c\to c^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{C}
such that the following diagram in
\mathsf{J}
commutes.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(c)}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(c^{\prime})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}

The object
j
of
\mathsf{J}
can either be an object of
\mathsf{C}
or an object of
\mathsf{D}
. We consider the two cases separately.

Case 1: Contractibility of
F{\downarrow}{c}
.
Let
j
be an object of
\mathsf{C}
, say
j=c
. Consider the object
(c,1_{c})
of
F{\downarrow}{c}
, where
1_{c}:F(c)\to c
is the identity morphism. Given another object
(c^{\prime},h)
in
F{\downarrow}{c}
, where
h:F(c^{\prime})=c^{\prime}\to c
, there is a unique morphism
(c^{\prime},h)\to(c,1_{c})
, namely
h:c^{\prime}\to c
, that makes the following diagram commute.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}(c^{\prime})}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}(c)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\;}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{c}}

So
(c,1_{c})
is the terminal object in
{F}{\downarrow}c
, and
{F}{\downarrow}{c}
is contractible by Lemma
2.27
.

Case 2: Contractibility of
F{\downarrow}{d}
.
Let
j
be an object of
\mathsf{D}
, say
j=d
. Let
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{D}}\in\hom_{\mathsf{D}}({L}{U}d,d)
be the counit at
d
. Let
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{J}}\in\hom_{\mathsf{J}}(Ud,d)
be the corresponding morphism in
\mathsf{J}
. We will show that
(Ud,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{J}})
is the terminal object in
F{\downarrow}d
. Let
(c,h)
be another object of
F{\downarrow}d
.

Then,
F(c)=c
, and
h\in\hom_{\mathsf{J}}(c,d)
. Let
h_{\mathsf{D}}\in\hom_{\mathsf{D}}(Lc,d)
be the corresponding morphism in
\mathsf{D}
. Because
(L,U)
is an adjoint pair, every
h_{\mathsf{D}}\in\hom(Lc,d)
factors through
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{D}}:LUd\to d
. That is, there exists a unique morphism
m:c\to Ud
such that the following diagram in
\mathsf{D}
commutes.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LUd}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(m)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\mathsf{D}}}

Thus,
m:c\to Ud
is the unique morphism such that the following diagram in
\mathsf{J}
commutes.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\mathsf{J}}}

So
m
is the unique morphism from
(c,h)
to
(Ud,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{J}})
in
\mathsf{J}
. So
(Ud,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{J}})
is the terminal object in
F{\downarrow}{d}
, and
F{\downarrow}{d}
is contractible (Lemma
2.27
).

In both cases,
F{\downarrow}j
is contractible. By Quillen’s Theorem A,
B\mathsf{C}\simeq B\mathsf{J}
(Theorem
2.25
). An analogous argument shows
B\mathsf{J}\simeq B\mathsf{D}
. ∎

5.1.2.
Graphs of profunctors arising from adjoint pairs

Let
L:\mathsf{C}\rightleftarrows\mathsf{D}:U
be an adjoint pair, let
P
be the associated profunctor, and let
\mathsf{G}
be the graph of the profunctor (Def.
2.34
). Recall that a morphism from
(c,d,\varphi)
to
(c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
is a pair of morphisms
(f,g)
of
f:c\to c^{\prime}
and
g:d\to d^{\prime}
forming the following square.

(14)

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

The morphism can equivalently be described as
(f,g)
that make the following diagrams in
\mathsf{C}
(left) and
\mathsf{D}
(right) commute. Note that by the adjointness of
(L,U)
, one of the following diagrams commute if the other one commutes as well.

(15)

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ug}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{C}}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lc^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{D}}}

When the profunctor arises from an adjoint pair, then the classifying space of
\mathsf{G}
is homotopy equivalent to that of
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
. The following theorem is analogous to Dowker duality via relational products (Theorem
3.25
).

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
{L}:\mathsf{C}\rightleftarrows\mathsf{D}:{U}
be an adjoint pair, let
P:\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}\times\mathsf{D}\to\text{Set}
be the associated profunctor, and let
\mathsf{G}
be the graph of
P
. Then,
B\mathsf{C}\simeq B\mathsf{G}\simeq B\mathsf{D}
.

Proof.

Let
F:\mathsf{G}\to\mathsf{C}
be the functor.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c,d,\varphi)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Fix an object
c_{*}
of
\mathsf{C}
, and consider the fiber
c_{*}{\downarrow}F
. We will show that
c_{*}{\downarrow}F
is contractible.
For clarity, let us remind ourselves of the objects and morphisms of
c_{*}{\downarrow}F
before proceeding with the proof.

•

Objects:
((c,d,\varphi),h)
, where
(c,d,\varphi)
is an object of
\mathsf{G}
and
h:c_{*}\to F(c,d,\varphi)
. Since
F(c,d,\varphi)=c
, we have that
h:c_{*}\to c
is a morphism in
\mathsf{C}
. The object
((c,d,\varphi),h)
will be drawn as the follows.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

Let
\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}\in\hom_{\mathsf{C}}(c,Ud)
be the morphism corresponding to
\varphi
. We can represent the object
((c,d,\varphi),h)
as the following diagram in
\mathsf{C}.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}

•

Morphisms:
(f,g):((c,d,\varphi),h)\to((c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),h^{\prime})
, where
(f,g):(c,d,\varphi)\to(c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{G}
such that the following diagram in
\mathsf{C}
commutes.

\hbox to120.2pt{\vbox to64.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.09949pt\lower-32.14091pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.8852pt}{12.9384pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{

Since
(f,g):(c,d,\varphi)\to(c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
is a morphism in
\mathsf{G}
, we must also have the square in Diagram
14
. All together, a morphism
(f,g):((c,d,\varphi),h)\to((c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),h^{\prime})
is a pair of morphisms
(f,g)
such that we have the following diagram

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

i.e., the following diagram in
\mathsf{C}
commutes.

(16)

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ug}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{C}}}

We now show that
c_{*}{\downarrow}{F}
is contractible by building natural transformations from the constant functor
C
on
c_{*}{\downarrow}{F}
to the identity functor on
c_{*}{\downarrow}{F}
. Let
\mathbbm{1}:c_{*}{{\downarrow}}F\to c_{*}{\downarrow}F
be the identity functor. To define the constant functor, let
\eta_{c_{*}}:c_{*}\to ULc_{*}
be the unit of the adjunction
(L,U)
. Let
\tilde{\eta}_{c_{*}}
be the corresponding heteromorphism in
P(c_{*},Lc_{*})
. Let
C
be the constant functor

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}{\downarrow}F}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}{\downarrow}F}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((c,d,\varphi),h)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((c_{*},Lc_{*},\tilde{\eta}_{c_{*}}),1_{c_{*}})}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),h^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((c_{*},Lc_{*},\tilde{\eta}_{c_{*}}),1_{c_{*}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{c_{*}},1_{Lc_{*}})}

We now define the natural transformation
\Psi:C\Rightarrow\mathbbm{1}
. For each object
((c,d,\varphi),h)
of
c_{*}{\downarrow}F
, let

\Psi_{((c,d,\varphi),h)}:C((c,d,\varphi),h)\to\mathbbm{1}((c,d,\varphi),h)

be the morphism

(h,k):((c_{*},Lc_{*},\tilde{\eta}_{c_{*}}),1_{c_{*}})\to((c,d,\varphi),h),

where
k:Lc_{*}\to d
is defined as follows. Since
\eta_{c_{*}}:c_{*}\to ULc_{*}
is the unit at
c_{*}
, given
\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}\circ h:c_{*}\to Ud
, there exists a unique morphism
k:Lc_{*}\to d
such that
\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}\circ h=U(k)\circ\eta_{c_{*}}
. Then, the following diagram in
\mathsf{C}
commutes, and
(h,k)
is a valid morphism in
c_{*}{\downarrow}F
.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ULc_{*}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{c_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathsf{C}}}

To check that
\Psi
is a natural transformation, consider any morphism
(f,g):((c,d,\varphi),h)\to((c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),h^{\prime})
in
c_{*}{\downarrow}{F}
and the diagram

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.01537pt}{-13.81589pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Note that
k^{\prime}:Lc_{*}\to d^{\prime}
is the unique morphism such that
\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{C}}\circ h^{\prime}=U(k^{\prime})\circ\eta_{c_{*}}
.

We now check that the above diagram commutes. Since
(f,g):((c,d,\varphi),h)\to((c^{\prime},d^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),h^{\prime})
is a morphism in
c_{*}{\downarrow}F
, we know that
h^{\prime}=f\circ h
.

The composition
(f,g)\circ(h,k)=(f\circ h,g\circ k)
corresponds to the following commutative diagram in
\mathsf{C}
.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ULc_{*}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ud^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ h=h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(g\circ k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{c_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{C}}}

Recall that
k^{\prime}:Lc_{*}\to d^{\prime}
is the unique morphism such that
\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathsf{C}}\circ h^{\prime}=U(k^{\prime})\circ\eta_{c_{*}}
. Thus, it must be the case that
k^{\prime}=g\circ k
. Thus, Diagram
17
commutes, and
\Psi
is a natural transformation.

Since natural transformations induce homotopies between the classifying spaces (Lemma
2.29
),
B\mathsf{}\mathbbm{1}\simeq B\mathsf{}C
. Thus,
B\mathsf{}(c_{*}{\downarrow}F)
is contractible. By Quillen’s Theorem A,
B\mathsf{G}\simeq B\mathsf{C}
. An analogous proof shows that
B\mathsf{G}\simeq B\mathsf{D}
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

Recall from Remark
2.35
that there are four different flavors of graphs of profunctors. Depending on how one chooses the graph, one may need a slightly different strategy for establishing contractibility of fibers. For example, one may need to string together two natural transformations, much like the proof of Theorem
3.25
.

Remark 5.4
.

One can also prove Theorem
5.2
by establishing that

\mathsf{G}\to\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{D}
is an instance of a Dowker relation as defined in
[
7
]
. One needs to establish the contractibility of the relevant fibers using the units and counits of the adjoint pair. Theorem
5.2
then follows from Theorem 4.5 in
[
7
]
.

Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
together show that if
{L}:\mathsf{C}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{D}:{U}
is an adjoint pair, then we have the following homotopy equivalences.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathsf{G}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathsf{D}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathsf{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

5.2.
Profunctors, cographs, graphs, and long exact sequences

In the previous section, we showed that
B\mathsf{C}\simeq B\mathsf{J}\simeq B\mathsf{G}\simeq B\mathsf{D}
when the profunctor arises from an adjoint pair between
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
. Here, we consider a general situation in which
P:\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}\times\mathsf{D}\to\text{Set}
is a profunctor that does not necessarily arise from an adjoint pair. We show that the homologies are related through a long exact sequence. The main technique is spectral sequences. The terms of the spectral sequences can be described as homologies of
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
with coefficients in specific functors as described below.

5.2.1.
Homologies of categories from profunctors

Given a profunctor
P:\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}\times\mathsf{D}\to\text{Set}
, we define functors
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}:\mathsf{C}\to\text{Ab}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}:\mathsf{D}\to\text{Ab}
that will be used to compute the homologies of
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
. The coefficient systems will be the homologies of heteromorphisms with either a fixed domain or a codomain. We briefly describe the homologies of such heteromorphisms before defining the coefficient systems.

Heteromorphisms with fixed domain or codomain.

Given a fixed object
c
of
\mathsf{C}
, the collection of heteromorphisms
P(c,\mathsf{D})
form a category. An object of
P(c,\mathsf{D})
is a pair
(\varphi,d)
representing a heteromorphism
\varphi:c\rightsquigarrow d
. A morphism
g
from
(\varphi,d)
to
(\varphi^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is a morphism
g:d\to d^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{D}
such that the following diagram commutes in the graph
\mathsf{G}
.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

Similarly, for a fixed object
d
of
\mathsf{D}
, the heteromorphisms
P(\mathsf{C},d)
form a category where the objects are
\varphi:c\rightsquigarrow d
and a morphism from
\varphi:c\rightsquigarrow d
to
\varphi^{\prime}:c^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow d
is a morphism
f:c\to c^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{C}
such that the following diagram commutes in
\mathsf{G}
.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

Later, when defining coefficient systems for computing the homologies of
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
, we will use the homologies of the categories
P(c,\mathsf{D})
and
P(\mathsf{C},d)
with constant coefficients. We provide an explicit description of the homology of
P(c,\mathsf{D})
as it will serve us well in the following section.

Let
A
be some fixed abelian group.
Let
\sigma
be a
q
-composable morphism in
P(c,\mathsf{D})
of the form

\sigma\quad:\quad(\varphi,d_{0})\xrightarrow{g_{0}}(\varphi_{1},d_{1})\xrightarrow{g_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{g_{q-1}}(\varphi_{q-1},d_{q}).

It represents the collection of heteromorphisms
\varphi,\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{q-1}
such that the following diagram commutes in the graph
\mathsf{G}
:

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{q-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{q-1}}

Since the above diagram commutes, we can equivalently represent
\sigma
by

\sigma\quad:\quad\hbox to232.67pt{\vbox to18.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 116.33186pt\lower-8.59027pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

To define the homologies of
P(c,\mathsf{D})
, first define the
q
-chains as

C_{q}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))=\bigoplus_{\sigma=(c\rightsquigarrow d_{0}\to\cdots\to d_{q})}A_{\sigma}

To define the boundary morphisms
\partial_{q}:C_{q}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))\to C_{q-1}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))
, first define

(18)

v_{j}(\sigma)=\begin{cases}c\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.65752pt\hskip-6.65752pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.65752pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-6.65752pt\raisebox{7.13991pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle g_{0}\varphi$}}\hskip-6.65752pt\hskip 6.65752pt}}\hskip-6.65752pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.3611pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 6.65752pt}}d_{1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}&\text{for }j=0\\
c\rightsquigarrow d_{0}\to\cdots d_{j-1}\xrightarrow{g_{j}g_{j-1}}d_{j+1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}&\text{for }0<j<q\\
c\rightsquigarrow d_{0}\to d_{1}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{g_{q-2}}d_{q-1}&\text{for }j=q.\end{cases}

We then define
\epsilon_{j}:C_{q}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))\to C_{q-1}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))
by setting

\epsilon_{j}\circ\iota_{\sigma}=\iota_{v_{j}(\sigma)}

for all
0\leq j\leq q
. Here, given any
\sigma=(c\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.00002pt\hskip-5.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.62987pt\raisebox{7.13991pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varphi$}}\hskip-2.62987pt\hskip 5.0pt}}\hskip-5.00002pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.3611pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 5.00002pt}}d_{0}\xrightarrow{g_{0}}\cdots\xrightarrow{g_{q-1}}d_{q})
, we use
\iota_{\sigma}:A_{\sigma}\to C_{q}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))
to denote the inclusion from the corresponding summand. Finally, define the boundary morphism
\partial_{q}:C_{q}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))\to C_{q-1}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))
by

\partial_{q}=\sum_{j=0}^{q}(-1)^{j}\epsilon_{j}\;.

We then have the chain complex

(19)

C_{\bullet}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))\,:\quad\cdots\to C_{n+1}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))\xrightarrow{\partial_{n+1}}C_{n}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))\xrightarrow{\partial_{n}}C_{n-1}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))\to\cdots\quad.

We refer to the homology of the resulting chain complex as the homology of
P(c,\mathsf{D})
, and we denote it by
H_{n}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))
. Note that this homology coincides with the singular homology of the classifying space of
P(c,\mathsf{D})
.

The homologies of
P(\mathsf{C},d)
can be defined analogously.

Coefficient systems from profunctors.

We now define coefficient systems on
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
. The homologies of
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
with these coefficients will be the entries of the spectral sequences in the following section.

Let
A
be a fixed abelian group. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q}:\mathsf{C}\to\text{Ab}
be the contravariant functor that sends
c
to
H_{q}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))
and sends
f:c\to c^{\prime}
to
f_{*}:H_{q}(P(c^{\prime},\mathsf{D}))\to H_{q}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))
. We can then compute the homology of
\mathsf{C}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q}
. According to Definition
2.30
,
H_{p}(\mathsf{C},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q})
is the homology of the chain complex

(20)

C_{\bullet}(\mathsf{C},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q}):\hskip 5.0pt\cdots\to\bigoplus_{\sigma\in N\mathsf{C}_{p+1}}H_{q}{P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D})}\to\bigoplus_{\sigma\in N\mathsf{C}_{p}}H_{q}{P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D})}\to\bigoplus_{\sigma\in N\mathsf{C}_{p-1}}H_{q}{P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D})}\to\cdots\;.

Here, we use
\sigma_{t}
to denote the target of
\sigma
.

Dually, let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p}:\mathsf{D}\to\text{Ab}
be the covariant functor that takes
d
to
H_{q}(P(\mathsf{C},d))
and sends
g:d\to d^{\prime}
to
g_{*}:H_{q}(P(\mathsf{C},d))\to H_{q}(P(\mathsf{C},d^{\prime}))
. Considering
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p}:D^{\textnormal{op}}\to\text{Ab}
as a contravariant functor from
D^{\textnormal{op}}
, the homology
H_{q}(\mathsf{D}^{\textnormal{op}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p})
is the homology of the chain complex

(21)

C_{\bullet}(\mathsf{D}^{\textnormal{op}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p}):\hskip 3.00003pt\cdots\to\bigoplus_{\tau\in N\mathsf{D}_{q+1}}H_{p}{P(\mathsf{C},\tau_{s})}\to\bigoplus_{\tau\in N\mathsf{D}_{q}}H_{p}{P(\mathsf{C},\tau_{s})}\to\bigoplus_{\tau\in N\mathsf{D}_{q-1}}H_{p}{P(\mathsf{C},\tau_{s})}\to\cdots\,.

Here,
\tau_{s}
denotes the source of
\tau
.

5.2.2.
Long exact sequences arising from profunctors

In this section, we establish a long exact sequence involving the homologies of the cograph
\mathsf{J}
that arise from profunctors. We will first construct spectral sequences whose terms are the homologies of categories
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p}
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
P:\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}\times\mathsf{D}\to\text{Set}
be a profunctor. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q}:\mathsf{C}\to\text{Ab}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p}:\mathsf{D}\to\text{Ab}
be the functors

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}}
Ab
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{q}(P(c,\mathsf{D}))}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{q}(P(c^{\prime},\mathsf{D}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
Ab
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{p}(P(\mathsf{C},d))}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{p}(P(\mathsf{C},d^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{*}}

There exist first quadrant spectral sequences

E^{\mathsf{C},2}_{p,q}=H_{p}(\mathsf{C},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q})\implies H_{*}

and

E^{\mathsf{D},2}_{p,q}=H_{q}(\mathsf{D}^{\textnormal{op}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p})\implies H_{*}

that converge to the same graded object.

Proof.

We show that the two spectral sequences arise from the same double complex. Let
\mathsf{J}
be the cograph of
P
and fix an abelian group
A
. For
p,q\geq 0
, let
E^{0}_{p,q}=\bigoplus_{\lambda}A_{\lambda}
, where
each
\lambda
is a composable morphism in
\mathsf{J}
of the form

(22)

\lambda\;:\quad c_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{p-1}}c_{p}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.00002pt\hskip-5.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.62987pt\raisebox{7.13991pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varphi$}}\hskip-2.62987pt\hskip 5.0pt}}\hskip-5.00002pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.3611pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 5.00002pt}}d_{0}\xrightarrow{g_{0}}d_{1}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{g_{q-1}}d_{q}.

The first
p
morphisms are in
\mathsf{C}
and the last
q
morphisms are in
\mathsf{D}
.

We now define the boundary morphisms among
E^{0}_{p,q}
’s. We first define the boundary morphisms
\delta_{p,q}:E^{0}_{p,q}\to E^{0}_{p-1,q}
that keep
q
fixed. Let
p>0
and
q\geq 0
. First, define face maps
u^{p,q}_{i}
(for
0\leq i\leq p
) that keep the morphisms in
\mathsf{D}
fixed as follows

(23)

u^{p,q}_{i}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{p-1}}c_{p}\rightsquigarrow d_{0}\to d_{1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}&\text{for }i=0\\
c_{0}\to\cdots\to c_{i-1}\xrightarrow{f_{i}f_{i-1}}c_{i+1}\to\cdots c_{p}\rightsquigarrow d_{0}\to d_{1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}&\text{for }0<i<p\\
c_{0}\to\cdots\to c_{p-1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 9.95319pt\hskip-9.95319pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 9.95317pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-9.95317pt\raisebox{7.75102pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varphi f_{p-1}$}}\hskip-9.95317pt\hskip 9.95317pt}}\hskip-9.95319pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.97221pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 9.95319pt}}d_{0}\to d_{1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}&\text{for }i=p.\end{cases}

We now define

\varepsilon_{i}^{p,q}:E^{0}_{p,q}\to E^{0}_{p-1,q}\quad\text{ for }0\leq i\leq p

by setting

\varepsilon^{p,q}_{i}\circ\iota_{\lambda}=\iota_{u^{p,q}_{i}\lambda}

for all
0\leq i\leq p
. Here,
\iota_{\lambda}:A_{\lambda}\to E^{0}_{p,q}
is the inclusion from the corresponding summand.

Define the boundary morphism
\delta_{p,q}:E^{0}_{p,q}\to E^{0}_{p-1,q}
by

\delta_{p,q}=\sum_{i=0}^{p}(-1)^{i}\varepsilon^{p,q}_{i}.

We now construct the boundary morphism
\delta:E^{0}_{p,q}\to E^{0}_{p,q-1}
that keeps
p
fixed. Let
p\geq 0
and
q>0
. Let us define the face maps
v^{p,q}_{j}
(for
0\leq j\leq q
) that keep the morphisms in
\mathsf{C}
fixed. Define

(24)

v^{p,q}_{j}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}c_{0}\to\cdots\to c_{p}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.65752pt\hskip-6.65752pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.65752pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-6.65752pt\raisebox{7.13991pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle g_{0}\varphi$}}\hskip-6.65752pt\hskip 6.65752pt}}\hskip-6.65752pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.3611pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 6.65752pt}}d_{1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}&\text{for }j=0\\
c_{0}\to\cdots\to c_{p}\rightsquigarrow d_{0}\to\cdots d_{j-1}\xrightarrow{g_{j}g_{j-1}}d_{j+1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}&\text{for }0<j<q\\
c_{0}\to\cdots\to c_{p}\rightsquigarrow d_{0}\to d_{1}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{g_{q-2}}d_{q-1}&\text{for }j=q.\end{cases}

We then define

\epsilon_{j}^{p,q}:E^{0}_{p,q}\to E^{0}_{p,q-1}\quad\text{ for }0\leq j\leq q

by setting

\epsilon^{p,q}_{j}\circ\iota_{\lambda}=\iota_{v^{p,q}_{j}\lambda}

for all
0\leq j\leq q
. Finally, define the boundary morphism
\partial_{p,q}:E^{0}_{p,q}\to E^{0}_{p,q-1}
by

\partial_{p,q}=\sum_{j=0}^{q}(-1)^{j}\epsilon^{p,q}_{j}.

The
E^{0}_{p,q}
’s fit together to form the following double complex
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
.

(25)

C_{\bullet,\bullet}\>:\hskip 40.00006pt\hbox to212.53pt{\vbox to135.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 106.26381pt\lower-67.8907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

We now show that the spectral sequences
E^{\mathsf{C},2}_{p,q}
and
E^{\mathsf{D},2}_{p,q}
are precisely the vertical and horizontal spectral sequences arising from
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
. For a fixed
\sigma
representing
c_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{p-1}}c_{p}
, let
E^{0}_{\sigma,q}=\bigoplus_{\tau}A_{\tau}
, where each
\tau
is of the form

\sigma\rightsquigarrow d_{0}\to d_{1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}.

Note that
E^{0}_{p,q}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in N\mathsf{C}_{p}}E^{0}_{\sigma,q}
. The
p^{\text{th}}
column of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
can be written as
C_{p,\bullet}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in N\mathsf{C}_{p}}C_{\sigma,\bullet}
, where

C_{\sigma,\bullet}\,:\hskip 30.00005pt\cdots\to E^{0}_{\sigma,q+1}\to E^{0}_{\sigma,q}\to E^{0}_{\sigma,q-1}\to\cdots.

The summand
C_{\sigma,\bullet}
coincide with the chain complex

C_{\bullet}(P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D}))\;:\hskip 30.00005pt\cdots\to C_{q+1}(P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D}))\to C_{q}(P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D}))\to C_{q-1}(P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D}))\to\cdots

from Equation
19
, where
\sigma_{t}
is the target of
\sigma
. So
C_{p,\bullet}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in N\mathsf{C}_{p}}C_{\bullet}(P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D}))
, and the homology of
C_{p,\bullet}
is
\bigoplus_{\sigma\in N\mathsf{C}_{p}}H_{q}(P(\sigma_{t},\mathsf{D})).
Thus, when we compute the homologies of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
with respect to the vertical differentials, we obtain the following first page, which we label
E^{\mathsf{C},1}
.

(26)

E^{\mathsf{C},1}\>:\hskip 40.00006pt\hbox to401.53pt{\vbox to171.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 200.76216pt\lower-85.68082pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

C_{\bullet}(\mathsf{C},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q})
in Equation

. Computing the homologies, we obtain the second page, which we label

On the other hand, consider computing the homologies of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
(Diagram
25
) with respect to the horizontal differentials. Fix a
q
, and consider the
q^{\text{th}}
row of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
:

C_{\bullet,q}\>:\quad\cdots\leftarrow E^{0}_{p-1,q}\xleftarrow{\delta_{p,q}}E^{0}_{p,q}\xleftarrow{\delta_{p+1,q}}E^{0}_{p+1,q}\leftarrow\cdots

Again, the
p^{\text{th}}
homology of the above chain complex is
\bigoplus_{\tau\in N\mathsf{D}_{q}}H_{p}(P(\mathsf{C},\tau_{s}))
, where
\tau_{s}
denotes the source of
\tau
. Computing the homologies of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
with respect to the horizontal differentials, we then obtain the following first page.

(28)

which is the spectral sequence
E^{\mathsf{D},2}_{p,q}
.

Since both
E^{\mathsf{C},2}_{p,q}
and
E^{\mathsf{D},2}_{p,q}
are first-quadrant spectral sequences arising from the double complex, they both converge to the homologies of the total complex of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
in Diagram
25

2
2

2

See
[
32
]
5.2.5 for convergence of bounded spectral sequences. Note that the filtration on
H_{*}
can be different for the two spectral sequences.
. ∎

The abutment
H_{*}
, fits into a long exact sequence of homologies of
\mathsf{C}
,
\mathsf{D}
, and the cograph
\mathsf{J}
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
P:\mathsf{C}^{\textnormal{op}}\times\mathsf{D}\to\text{Set}
be a profunctor. Let
\mathsf{J}
be the cograph of
P
. The abutment of the spectral sequences

E^{\mathsf{C},2}_{p,q}=H_{p}(\mathsf{C},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{C}}^{q})\implies H_{*}

and

E^{\mathsf{D},2}_{p,q}=H_{q}(\mathsf{D}^{\textnormal{op}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{D}}^{p})\implies H_{*}

fit into a long exact sequence

(30)

\cdots\to H_{n+1}(\mathsf{J})\to H_{n}\to H_{n}(\mathsf{C})\,\oplus\,H_{n}(\mathsf{D})\to H_{n}(\mathsf{J})\to H_{n-1}\to\cdots.

The proof first extends the double complex
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
from Diagram
25
into a larger double complex. We then obtain the desired long exact sequence from a short exact sequence of complexes.

Proof.

Let us extend
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
from Diagram
8
to a larger double complex by defining

E^{0}_{p,-1}=\bigoplus_{c_{0}\to\cdots\to c_{p}}A\,,\hskip 40.00006ptE^{0}_{-1,q}=\bigoplus_{d_{0}\to\cdots\to d_{q}}A\,.

To extend the differentials, recall from Equations
23
and
24
the face maps
u^{p,q}_{i}
’s (defined for
p>0,\,q\geq 0
, and
0\leq i\leq p
) and
v^{p,q}_{j}
(defined for
p\geq 0,\,q>0
, and
0\leq j\leq q
). We extend the face maps to lower indices as follows.

For
p=0
and
q\geq 0
, define

u^{0,q}_{0}(c_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.00002pt\hskip-5.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.62987pt\raisebox{7.13991pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varphi$}}\hskip-2.62987pt\hskip 5.0pt}}\hskip-5.00002pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.3611pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 5.00002pt}}d_{0}\to\cdots\to d_{q})=d_{0}\to\cdots\to d_{q}.

Similarly, for
p\geq 0
and
q=0
, define

v^{p,0}_{0}(c_{0}\to\cdots\to c_{p}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.00002pt\hskip-5.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.62987pt\raisebox{7.13991pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varphi$}}\hskip-2.62987pt\hskip 5.0pt}}\hskip-5.00002pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-3.3611pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 5.00002pt}}d_{0})=c_{0}\to\cdots\to c_{p}.

For
p\geq 0
, let
u^{p,-1}_{i}
be the usual face maps on
c_{0}\to c_{1}\to\cdots\to c_{p}
for
0\leq i\leq p
. Similarly, given
q\geq 0
, let
v^{-1,q}_{j}
be the usual face maps on
d_{0}\to d_{1}\to\cdots\to d_{q}
for
0\leq j\leq q
from Equation
1
.

We now proceed to define morphisms

\displaystyle\partial_{p,0}

\displaystyle:E^{0}_{p,0}\to E^{0}_{p,-1}\,\text{ for }p\geq 0

\displaystyle\partial_{-1,q}

\displaystyle:E^{0}_{-1,q}\to E^{0}_{-1,q-1}\,\text{ for }q\geq 0

\displaystyle\delta_{0,q}

\displaystyle:E^{0}_{0,q}\to E^{0}_{-1,q}\,\text{ for }q\geq 0

\displaystyle\delta_{p,-1}

\displaystyle:E^{0}_{p,-1}\to E^{0}_{p-1,-1}\,\text{ for }p\geq 0\,.

In the following,
\lambda
will be morphisms in
\mathsf{J}
corresponding to the summands of
E^{0}_{p,q}
, and
\iota_{\lambda}:A_{\lambda}\to E^{0}_{p,q}
denotes the inclusion from the corresponding summand.

\partial_{p,0}:E^{0}_{p,0}\to E^{0}_{p,-1}
for
p\geq 0
: First define
\epsilon_{0}^{p,0}:E^{0}_{p,0}\to E^{0}_{p,-1}
by setting

\epsilon^{p,0}_{0}\circ\iota_{\lambda}=\iota_{v^{p,0}_{0}\lambda}.

Define
\partial_{p,0}:E^{0}_{p,0}\to E^{0}_{p,-1}
by

\partial_{p,0}=\epsilon^{p,0}_{0}.

\partial_{-1,q}:E^{0}_{-1,q}\to E^{0}_{-1,q-1}
for
q\geq 0
: Define
\epsilon_{j}^{-1,q}:E^{0}_{-1,q}\to E^{0}_{-1,q-1}
for
0\leq j\leq q
by setting

\epsilon^{-1,q}_{j}\circ\iota_{\lambda}=\iota_{v^{-1,q}_{j}\lambda}.

Define
\partial_{-1,q}:E^{0}_{-1,q}\to E^{0}_{-1,q-1}
by

\partial_{-1,q}=\sum_{j=0}^{q}(-1)^{j}\epsilon^{-1,q}_{j}.

\delta_{0,q}:E^{0}_{0,q}\to E^{0}_{-1,q}
for
q\geq 0
: First define
\varepsilon_{0}^{0,q}:E^{0}_{0,q}\to E^{0}_{-1,q}
by setting

\varepsilon^{0,q}_{0}\circ\iota_{\lambda}=\iota_{u^{0,q}_{0}\lambda}.

Define
\delta_{0,q}:E^{0}_{0,q}\to E^{0}_{-1,q}
by

\delta_{0,q}=\varepsilon^{0,q}_{0}.

\delta_{p,-1}:E^{0}_{p,-1}\to E^{0}_{p-1,-1}
for
p\geq 0
: Define
\varepsilon_{i}^{p,-1}:E^{0}_{p,-1}\to E^{0}_{p-1,-1}
for
0\leq i\leq p
by setting

\varepsilon^{p,-1}_{i}\circ\iota_{\lambda}=\iota_{u^{p,-1}_{i}\lambda}.

Define
\delta_{p,-1}:E^{0}_{p,-1}\to E^{0}_{p-1,-1}
by

\delta_{p,-1}=\sum_{i=0}^{p}(-1)^{i}\varepsilon^{p,-1}_{i}.

The
E^{0}_{p,q}
, for
p,q\geq-1
, now come together to form the following larger double complex.

(31)

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdots}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{E^{0}_{-1,2}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{0,2}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{1,2}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{2,2}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{E^{0}_{-1,1}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{0,1}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{1,1}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{2,1}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{E^{0}_{-1,0}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{0,0}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{1,0}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{2,0}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{0}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{E^{0}_{0,-1}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{E^{0}_{1,-1}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{E^{0}_{2,-1}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{-1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{2,3}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{-1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0,2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{0,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{2,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{3,2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{-1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{2,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{3,1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{-1,0}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0,0}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,0}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{2,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{3,0}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{0,-1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,-1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2,-1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{3,-1}}

Let
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
denote the double complex consisting of
p,q\geq 0
as before. Let
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,\bullet}
denote the larger double complex including
p=-1
and
q=-1
. In Diagram
31
,
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
is indicated in black, and
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,\bullet}
refers to the entire diagram, including the gray entries. Let
\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet}
denote the leftmost column of Diagram
31
and let
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}
denote the bottom row of Diagram
31
, both shown in gray. Let
\text{Tot}(C)_{\bullet}
and
\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet}
each denote the total chain complex of
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
and
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,\bullet}
. Explicitly,
\text{Tot}(C)_{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}C_{p,q}
and
\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\tilde{C}_{p,q}
.

There is a short exact sequence of complexes

0\to\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}\oplus\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet}\to\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet}\to\text{Tot}(C)_{\bullet}\to 0,

which results in the long exact sequence

\cdots\to H_{n+1}(\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}\oplus\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet})\to H_{n}(\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet})\to H_{n}(\text{Tot}(C)_{\bullet})\to H_{n}(\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}\oplus\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet})\to\cdots.

Note that
\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}
is the chain complex of
H_{n}(\mathsf{C})
, and
\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet}
is the chain complex of
H_{n}(\mathsf{D})
. So

H_{n}(\tilde{C}_{\bullet,-1}\oplus\tilde{C}_{-1,\bullet})=H_{n}(\mathsf{C})\oplus H_{n}(\mathsf{D}).

Furthermore, one can check that
\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet}
satisfies

H_{n}(\text{Tot}(\tilde{C})_{\bullet})=H_{n+1}(\mathsf{J}).

Lastly, since
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
is a first quadrant double complex,
E^{\mathsf{C},2}_{p,q}
converges to
H_{*}=H_{n}(\text{Tot}(C)_{\bullet})
.

All together, we obtain the long exact sequence in Equation
30
.
∎
